Global Martingale Entropy Solutions to the Stochastic Isentropic Euler Equations
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Feimin Huang, Danli Wang

Abstract
We establish the existence and compactness of global martingale entropy solutions with finite relative-energy for the stochastically forced system of isentropic Euler equations governed by a general pressure law. To achieve these, a stochastic compensated compactness framework in L p L^{p} is developed to overcome the difficulty that the uniform L ∞ L^{\infty} bound for the stochastic approximate solutions is unavailable, owing to the stochastic forcing term. The convergence of the vanishing viscosity method is established by employing the stochastic compactness framework, along with careful uniform estimates of the stochastic approximate solutions, to obtain the existence of global martingale entropy solutions with finite relative-energy. In particular, in the polytropic pressure case for all adiabatic exponents, we prove that the global solutions satisfy the local mechanical energy inequality when the initial data are only required to have finite relative-energy (while the higher moment estimates for entropy are not required here, as needed in the earlier work). Higher-order relative energy estimates for approximate solutions are also derived to establish the entropy inequality for more convex entropy pairs and to then prove the compactness of solutions to the stochastic isentropic Euler system. The stochastic compensated compactness framework and the uniform estimate techniques for approximate solutions developed in this paper should be useful in the study of other similar problems.



Keywords:
Stochastic isentropic Euler equations, martingale entropy solutions,
positive far-field density, existence, compactness, uniform estimates, tightness,
stochastic
L^{p}
compensated compactness,
entropy analysis, relative-energy, stochastic parabolic approximation

MSC (2020):
35L65, 35Q31, 60H15, 60G57, 76N10

1
Introduction

We are concerned with the global existence and dynamic properties
of solutions of the Cauchy problem for
the stochastically forced system of isentropic Euler equations:

\begin{cases}{\rm d}\rho+\partial_{x}m\,{\rm d}t=0,\\[2.84526pt]
{\rm d}m+\partial_{x}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}t=\Phi(\rho,m)\,{\rm d}W(t),\\[2.84526pt]
(\rho,m)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},m_{0}),\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
\rho
is the density,
m
is the momentum,
P(\rho)
is the pressure,

W
is a cylindrical Wiener process,
the noise coefficient
\Phi
satisfies a suitable growth condition,
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
When
\rho>0
,
u=\frac{m}{\rho}
is the velocity.

The pressure-density relation
P=P(\rho)
under consideration in this paper
is governed by a general pressure law,
obeying conditions (
2.2
)–(
2.7
) as described in §2, which ensures that
system (
1.1
) is strictly hyperbolic and genuinely nonlinear when
\rho>0
,
among others. A particular case is the
\gamma
-law, which is the typical pressure law
for the class of polytropic gases:

P(\rho)=\kappa\rho^{\gamma}

(1.2)

with adiabatic exponent
\gamma>1
for some constant
\kappa>0
.

When
\Phi(\rho,m)\equiv 0
, system (
1.1
) reduces
to the deterministic isentropic Euler equations:

\begin{cases}{\rm d}\rho+\partial_{x}m\,{\rm d}t=0,\\[2.84526pt]
{\rm d}m+\partial_{x}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}t=0,\\[2.84526pt]
(\rho,m)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},m_{0}).\end{cases}

(1.3)

System (
1.3
) governed by a general pressure law was
first analyzed
by Chen-LeFloch
[
12
]
, which was extended by
Chen-LeFloch
[
13
]
to deal with more general pressure functions.
Most recently, Chen et al.
[
11
]
studied the compressible Euler-Poisson equations
for a general pressure law allowing different power laws near the vacuum and the far field, in which
a special weak entropy pair is constructed to obtain the uniform higher integrability
of the velocity such that the
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework for the general pressure law can be established.
For the polytropic gas, more results have been obtained.
The global existence of
L^{\infty}
entropy solutions
for (
1.3
) was established
in DiPerna
[
23
]
for the case
\gamma=\frac{2N+3}{2N+1}
for integer

N\geq 2
, Chen
[
8
]
and Ding-Chen-Luo
[
22
]

for the general case
\gamma\in\left(1,\frac{5}{3}\right]
,
Lions-Perthame-Tadmor
[
35
]
for the case
\gamma\geq 3
,
and Lions-Perthame-Souganidis
[
34
]

to close the gap
\gamma\in(\frac{5}{3},3)
and simplify
the proof for
\gamma>1
.
The isothermal case
\gamma=1
was treated by Huang-Wang
[
28
]
.
The above results are based on the
L^{\infty}

compensated compactness method,
in which the invariant region method is used
to obtain the uniform
L^{\infty}
bound for the parabolic approximation.
Chen-Perepelitsa
[
14
]
constructed a general compensated compactness
framework in
L^{p}
to prove the vanishing viscosity
limit of the Navier-Stokes equations to the isentropic Euler equations
with finite-energy initial data relative to the different end-states at infinity
for all
\gamma>1
.
In this
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework,
the
L^{\infty}
uniform bound provided by the invariant region method
was replaced by some uniform higher-integrability estimates
since the Navier-Stokes equations do not admit natural invariant regions.
Instead of
L^{\infty}
entropy solutions, the finite-energy solutions of the isentropic Euler equations
for the case
\gamma>1
was obtained in
[
14
]
,
even for global spherically symmetric solutions for the multidimensional
case for
1<\gamma\leq 3
in
[
15
]
,
which extended the result in LeFloch-Westdickenberg
[
33
]
for
1<\gamma\leq\frac{5}{3}
.
Recently, the compensated compactness framework established
in Chen-Perepelitsa
[
14
]
was applied
by Chen-Schrecker
[
16
]
to study the existence
of globally-defined entropy solutions to the Euler equations
for transonic nozzle flows with general cross-sectional areas
that may have different end-states for all the case
\gamma>1
.

For stochastically forced systems of hyperbolic conservation laws,
there exist only few results.
Kim
[
30
]
studied strong solutions of stochastic quasilinear
symmetric hyperbolic systems.
Recently, measured-valued solutions of the stochastic compressible Euler equations
were investigated in Hofmanová-Koley-Sarkar
[
27
]
.
To our knowledge, the only paper that considered entropy solutions to
the stochastic Euler equations
is Berthelin-Vovelle
[
3
]
, in which they established the
global existence of martingale entropy solutions to the stochastically
forced system of isentropic Euler
equations (
1.1
)
for the polytropic pressure case
on the one-dimensional torus.
In this paper
[
3
]
, the compensated compactness technique of the deterministic
case
[
34
]
was adapted to
the stochastic case as in Feng-Nualart
[
25
]

for the stochastic scalar conservation laws.
Because of the stochastic forcing term
\Phi(\rho,m){\rm d}W(t)
,
the uniform
L^{\infty}
bounds for the parabolic approximate solutions
cannot be obtained.
To overcome this difficulty, they established some higher moment estimates based on
careful analysis of entropies, thanks to the explicit formula
of the entropy kernel. However, the price to pay is that the initial data
are required to satisfy corresponding higher moment estimates.

In this paper, our aim is to establish
the existence and compactness of global
martingale entropy
solutions with finite relative-energy to the stochastic isentropic Euler
system (
1.1
) governed by the general pressure law,
when the initial data allow for a positive far-field density.
In the case that the initial density tends to zero at the far-field,
martingale entropy solutions with finite energy are obtained.
Compared to the result by Berthelin-Vovelle
[
3
]
,
our initial data are only required to satisfy the finite relative-energy.
The main difficulty is that the uniform
L^{\infty}
bound
for the parabolic approximate solutions is unavailable.
Thus, we develop the stochastic version of compensated compactness framework
in
L^{p}
,
which is the extension of the deterministic ones
in Chen-Perepelitsa
[
14
]
and Chen et al.
[
11
]
.
Moreover, we also apply this stochastic
compensated compactness framework in
L^{p}
to study
the stochastic isentropic Euler system (
1.1
)
governed by the
\gamma
-law,
for which we obtain the global martingale entropy solutions
that satisfy the local mechanical energy inequality in the sense of distributions.

Furthermore, when the initial data have a finite higher-order relative energy,
we show that the corresponding solutions also retain the finite higher-order relative energy.
This property allows us to derive the entropy inequality
for a broader class of convex entropy pairs,
including the mechanical energy and its associated flux in the general pressure law case.
These results are crucial, as they also enable us to establish the compactness of the sequence of martingale entropy solutions to the stochastic isentropic Euler
equations (
1.1
).

Some new difficulties and strategies to handle them are as follows:
To overcome the difficulty that the uniform
L^{\infty}
bound for the parabolic
approximation is unavailable,
we develop the stochastic version of
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework
as in the deterministic case, the basis of which is the uniform energy estimate
and some uniform higher integrability estimates for the parabolic
approximation (see §
3.2
).
However, to establish these uniform higher integrability estimates,
we cannot use the infinite-dimensional
Itô formula, since the transformations are not in
C^{2}
, not even usual
functions of the underlying stochastic process.
We deal with this problem by obtaining strong solutions with high regularity
(see Theorem
3.1
)
such that the parabolic approximation
equations (
3.1
)
hold in the classical strong sense.
Then we can fix any space variable
x

and use the finite-dimensional Itô formula
to establish the uniform higher integrability estimates.
Additionally, to obtain the global strong solutions of
the parabolic approximation
equations (
3.1
), we need to derive the

L^{\infty}
bound.
In the deterministic case, the invariant region method is a powerful tool
to derive the
L^{\infty}
bound.
To make the invariant region method applicable in the stochastic case,
on one hand, inspired by Berthelin-Vovelle
[
3
]
,
we assume that the noise coefficient is compactly supported
(see (
3.4
));
on the other hand, it is also required that the parabolic approximation
equations (
3.1
) hold in the classical strong sense
and the finite-dimensional Itô formula
can be applied (see Proposition
8.3
).

After establishing the well-posedness of the parabolic approximation equations (
3.1
),
as in the deterministic case, we employ the stochastic compactness method
to pass to the limit.
A usual way is to apply the Jakubowski-Skorokhod representation theorem,
which is different from the deterministic case,
gives us the almost sure Skorokhod representation,
i.e.
,
a sequence of new random variables on a new probability space that have the same laws
as the original ones and almost surely
converge (see Proposition
4.3
).
This method is powerful;
however, it also brings us additional difficulties,
i.e.
,
we need to be able to identify those new random variables when necessary.
More specifically, to establish the stochastic version of
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework,
we need to introduce a random Young measure,
as in the deterministic case, to pass to the limit
in the nonlinear terms.
Therefore, it is significant to identify that the Skorokhod representation of
the random Young measure associated with the parabolic approximation
is a Dirac mass and also a random Young measure associated
with the corresponding Skorokhod representation of the parabolic approximation.
Thanks to the integrability of the parabolic approximation, the random Young measure
here is
L^{r}
-random Dirac mass with
r\geq 1
.
The fact that the random Young measure is an
L^{r}
-random Dirac mass
is uniquely determined by its law (see
[
3
, Proposition 4.5]
).
Thus, we can identify the Skorokhod representation of the random Young
measure (see Lemma
5.7
).

In the procedure of establishing the stochastic version of
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework,
a key step is
to derive the tightness of random entropy dissipation measures
in some negative-order Sobolev space (see Lemma
5.6
).
Here the tightness should be derived under the Skorokhod representation of the parabolic
approximation (see Proposition
4.3
).
However, this can not be directly done and is difficult, since the Skorokhod representation
is a weak solution of the parabolic approximation equations (
3.1
).
Instead, we first obtain the tightness of random entropy dissipation measures under the original
parabolic approximation in some negative-order Sobolev space (see Proposition
5.2
),
which implies the corresponding tightness of random entropy dissipation measures
under the Skorokhod representation by using the equality of laws
(see the proof of Lemma
5.6
).

For the general pressure law case, the explicit formula of entropy is unavailable
so that the following additional difficulties arise:

(i) To establish suitable uniform estimates used to obtain enough compactness,
the method used in the deterministic polytropic pressure case,
i.e.
,
deriving the uniform higher integrability estimates for the density and the velocity
by utilizing an appropriate (non-convex) entropy with a special generating function,
cannot be directly applied, since there is no precise formula for the entropy kernel.
Furthermore, it is not clear how the higher moment estimates can be established
for the parabolic approximations and the entropy pairs
as in Berthelin-Vovelle
[
3
]

by using the asymptotic expansion of the entropy kernel.
In fact, these estimates in
[
3
]
are based on the fact that the entropy kernel
has an explicit formula, which allows for careful analysis and calculation by
choosing different generating functions.
To overcome this difficulty, we employ the special entropy pair
constructed in
[
11
]
to establish the higher integrability
of the velocity.

(ii) In the step of deriving the tightness of random entropy dissipation measures
in some negative-order Sobolev space,
the compactness that we can obtain is too weak to apply
the classical div-curl lemma in
[
37
]
.
However, thanks to the higher integrability of the density and the velocity established above,
we are able to show the equi-integrability,
so that we are able to use the generalized
div-curl lemma (see Lemma
A.12
).
In fact, due to the generality of the pressure and the lack of an explicit formula for the entropy kernel,
only some rough bounds on the entropy pairs are obtained.

(iii) For the reduction of the Young measure, since the uniform
L^{\infty}
bounds
for the parabolic approximation
are unavailable, the reduction technique for the bounded supported Young measure
introduced in
[
8
,
22
,
23
,
34
]

can not be applied.
In fact, in the deterministic case,
[
14
]
already encountered such a problem;
thanks to the precise structure of the entropy kernel for the polytropic pressure,
it can be shown first that every connected subset in the support of the Young measure
is bounded and then apply the reduction argument in
[
8
,
22
,
23
,
34
]
,
which is the key idea in
[
14
]
.
For the general pressure law case, this idea in
[
14
]

cannot be used directly.
We employ the reduction framework established in
[
11
]

for the Young measure with unbounded support and for the general pressure law,
which is based on a careful analysis of
the singularities of the fractional derivatives of the entropy kernel.

We also stress that, compared to the result in
[
3
]
,
the initial data here are only required to have finite (relative) energy
so that we do not need to establish the higher moment estimates for entropy pairs.
These improvements are essential since
we can expect to extend the one-dimensional result in this paper to
the multidimensional spherically symmetric case,
utilizing the so-established stochastic version of
compensated compactness framework in
L^{p}
.
This is out of the scope of this paper and will be addressed in our further work.

The organization of this paper is as follows:
In §
3.1
,
the main result is Theorem
3.1
,
which shows that there exists a unique strong solution to the parabolic approximate
equations (
3.1
).
We postpone the proof of Theorem
3.1

to §
8
, in which we use
a different method to prove the well-posedness of the parabolic approximate
equations (
3.1
)
and obtain slightly better results (compared to
[
3
]
),
i.e.
, almost surely,
the approximate density has a positive lower bound and the parabolic approximate solution
has an
L^{\infty}
bound.
The proof of Theorem
3.1

is based on the cut-off technique,
i.e.
, we first prove the existence
and uniqueness for the cut-off
equations (see
Theorem
8.1
)
and then remove the cut-off operator to obtain the unique global solution.
In §
3.2
,
the higher integrability estimates for the parabolic approximate solution are established.

In §
4
, we pass to the limit from the parabolic approximation
based on the compactness method.
In the stochastic setting, a usual way is to apply the Skorokhod representation theorem.
However, this is restricted to the Polish space.
Some of the spaces that we work with are not metric spaces,
thus we need to apply the Jakubowski-Skorokhod representation theorem
[
29
]
,
which provides a generalization of the classical Skorokhod representation theorem
to the sub-polish spaces that are not metrizable.

In §
5
,
we establish the stochastic version of compensated compactness framework in
L^{p}
.
The uniform higher integrability estimates obtained
in §
3.2

enable us to use the stochastic-version generalized Murat’s lemma
(see Lemma
A.9
) to establish the tightness
in some negative-order Sobolev space.
Note that, in this step, it does not require the higher moment estimates
as in
[
3
]
(where they required the generating
function
\psi=\xi^{2p}
in
\eta^{\psi}
with
p\geq 4+\frac{1}{2\theta}
),
but only need the estimates in Propositions

3.1
–
3.2
.
Having this tightness at hand, we apply the
generalized div-curl lemma
to derive the Tartar commutation relation, under which we can prove that,
almost surely,
the limit random Young measure is either concentrated on the vacuum region
or reduced to a Dirac mass.

In §
6
, we perform the limit to obtain the martingale entropy solutions
with finite relative-energy of the stochastic isentropic Euler
system (
1.1
) with
the general pressure law.
The uniform higher integrability estimates obtained
in §
3.2

provide enough equi-integrability, which enables us to perform
the limit and derive the energy estimates
and
the entropy inequality for the stochastic isentropic Euler system (
1.1
).
Furthermore, we establish the higher-order relative energy estimate for the solutions,
under the condition that the initial data have the finite higher-order relative energy,
which enables us to derive the entropy inequality for the solutions
satisfied by a broader class of entropy pairs,
including the mechanical energy-energy flux.
Then, as a direct application of the stochastic-version compensated compactness
framework in
L^{p}
, we obtain the compactness of the global martingale entropy
solutions to the stochastic isentropic Euler
system (
1.1
) governed by
the general
pressure law.

In §
7
, we study the stochastic isentropic Euler
system (
1.1
)
in the polytropic pressure case for all
\gamma>1
,
the results of which for
\gamma\leq 3
are covered by the general pressure law case.
Moreover, thanks to the explicit formulas of entropy pairs, we obtain better results
in this case.
In particular, constructing suitable approximate cut-off
generating functions,
we succeed in deriving the local entropy inequality
corresponding to the mechanical energy;
see Theorems
7.1

and
7.5
.
When the initial data have the finite higher-order relative energy,
we
obtain the entropy inequality for
the convex entropy pairs with generating functions of sub-cubic growth
and establish the compactness of the martingale entropy solution sequence
to the stochastic isentropic Euler
system (
1.1
)
for all adiabatic exponents
\gamma>1
.

2
Mathematical Problems and Main Theorems

In this section, we discuss some basic properties of the stochastic isentropic Euler equations,
set up our mathematical problems, and present the main theorems of this paper.

2.1
Stochastic Isentropic Euler Equations

System (
1.1
) can be written as

{\rm d}U+\partial_{x}F(U){\rm d}t=\Psi(U){\rm d}W(t),

(2.1)

where

U=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
m\end{pmatrix},\quad F(U)=\begin{pmatrix}m\\
\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\end{pmatrix},\quad\Psi(U)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\Phi(U)\end{pmatrix}.

(2.2)

The pressure-density relation under consideration in this paper is governed by the following general pressure law
obeying the two conditions:

(i)

The strict hyperbolicity and genuine nonlinearity of
system
(
1.1
)
are required when
\rho>0
:

P^{\prime}(\rho)>0,\quad 2P^{\prime}(\rho)+\rho P^{\prime\prime}(\rho)>0\qquad\text{ for \ $\rho>0$}

(2.3)

and
P(\rho)\in C^{1}(\left[0,\infty\right))\cap C^{4}(0,\infty)
.

(ii)

There exist constants
\rho^{\star}>\rho_{\star}>0
such that, for some
1<\gamma_{2}\leq\gamma_{1}<3
,

\displaystyle P(\rho)=\kappa_{1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}}(1+P_{1}(\rho))\qquad\text{ for \ $0\leq\rho<\rho_{\star}$},

(2.4)

\displaystyle P(\rho)=\kappa_{2}\rho^{\gamma_{2}}(1+P_{2}(\rho))\qquad\text{ for \ $\rho^{\star}\leq\rho<\infty$},

(2.5)

with
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
and
P_{1}(\rho),P_{2}(\rho)\in C^{4}(0,\infty)

satisfying

\displaystyle|P_{1}^{(n)}(\rho)|\leq C_{\star}\rho^{\gamma_{1}-1-n}\qquad\text{for $n=0,1,\cdots,4$, and $0<\rho<\rho_{\star}$},

(2.6)

\displaystyle|P_{2}^{(n)}(\rho)|\leq C^{\star}\rho^{-\flat-n}\qquad\,\,\,\,\text{for $n=0,1,\cdots,4$, and $\rho^{\star}\leq\rho<\infty$},

(2.7)

where
\flat>0
,
C_{\star}>0
depends only on
\rho_{\star}
,
and
C^{\star}>0
depends only on
\rho^{\star}
.

A particular case is the
\gamma
-law (
1.2
)
with adiabatic exponent
\gamma>1
for some constant
\kappa>0
.
Without loss of generality, constant
\kappa
in (
1.2
)
can be chosen, by scaling,
as
\kappa=\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{4\gamma}
.

Denote the sound speed by

c(\rho)=\sqrt{P^{\prime}(\rho)}.

(2.8)

Condition (
2.3
) ensures that, away from the vacuum, system
(
1.1
)
is strictly hyperbolic and
admits two genuinely nonlinear characteristic fields associated with two distinct
wave speeds:

\lambda_{1}=\frac{m}{\rho}-c(\rho),\qquad\lambda_{2}=\frac{m}{\rho}+c(\rho).

(2.9)

At the vacuum
\rho=0
,
c(0)=0
, and the wave speeds coincide
so that the strict hyperbolicity of system (
2.1
) fails.
Corresponding two Riemann invariants are

w_{1}=\frac{m}{\rho}-\mathcal{K}(\rho),\qquad w_{2}=\frac{m}{\rho}+\mathcal{K}(\rho),

(2.10)

where

\mathcal{K}(\rho):=\int_{0}^{\rho}\frac{\sqrt{P^{\prime}(y)}}{y}\,{\rm d}y.

(2.11)

2.2
Entropy Functions

An entropy-entropy flux pair (or entropy pair, for short) of
system (
1.1
),
equivalently system (
2.1
),
is a pair of functions
(\eta,q):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\nabla q(U)=\nabla\eta(U)\nabla F(U),

(2.12)

where
\nabla=(\partial_{\rho},\partial_{m})
.
Furthermore,
(\eta,q)(\rho,m)
is called a weak entropy pair
if
\eta
vanishes at the vacuum:
\rho=0
.

When
\rho>0
, we denote
u=\frac{m}{\rho}
as the velocity
and rewrite equations (
2.12
)
as the following wave equation for entropy
\eta
:

\partial_{\rho}^{2}\eta-\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\rho)^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}\eta=0,

which is degenerate when
\rho\to 0
, where
\mathcal{K}
is defined in (
2.11
) above.

As indicated in
[
23
,
35
,
12
,
13
]
,
any weak entropy pair can be expressed by a corresponding generating function
\psi
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\psi}(\rho,u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\rho,u,s)\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s,\quad q^{\psi}(\rho,u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(\rho,u,s)\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s,

(2.13)

where the entropy kernel
\chi(\rho,u,s)
is the solution of the singular Cauchy problem:

\begin{cases}\partial_{\rho}^{2}\chi-\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\rho)^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}\chi=0,\\
\chi|_{\rho=0}=0,\quad\partial_{\rho}\chi|_{\rho=0}=\delta_{u=s},\end{cases}

(2.14)

and the corresponding entropy flux kernel
\sigma(\rho,u,s)
satisfies

\begin{cases}\partial_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma-u\chi)-\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\rho)^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}(\sigma-u\chi)=0,\\
(\sigma-u\chi)|_{\rho=0}=0,\quad\partial_{\rho}(\sigma-u\chi)|_{\rho=0}=0.\end{cases}

Note that equation (
2.14
) is invariant under the Galilean transformation
(
cf.

[
12
]
), which implies that

\chi(\rho,u,s)=\chi(\rho,u-s,0)=\chi(\rho,0,s-u)
.
Therefore, we write
\chi(\rho,u,s)=\chi(\rho;u-s)
when no confusion arises.

In the polytropic pressure case (
1.2
), any weak entropy pair can be expressed
by the explicit formula via a generating function
\psi

(
cf.

[
22
,
23
,
35
]
):

\displaystyle\eta^{\psi}(\rho,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\rho^{2\theta}-(u-s)^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+}\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\rho\int_{-1}^{1}\psi(u+\rho^{\theta}s)[1-s^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+}\,{\rm d}s,

\displaystyle q^{\psi}(\rho,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta s+(1-\theta)u)[\rho^{2\theta}-(u-s)^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+}\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\rho\int_{-1}^{1}(u+\theta\rho^{\theta}s)\psi(u+\rho^{\theta}s)[1-s^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+}\,{\rm d}s,

through the entropy kernal
\chi(\rho,u,s)
and entropy flux kernel

\sigma(\rho,u,s)
:

\chi(\rho,u,s)=\chi(\rho;s-u)=[\rho^{2\theta}-(s-u)^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+},\qquad\sigma(\rho,u,s)=(\theta s+(1-\theta)u)[\rho^{2\theta}-(u-s)^{2}]^{\lambda}_{+}

where
\lambda=\frac{3-\gamma}{2(\gamma-1)}>-\frac{1}{2},\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
, and
[f]_{+}:=\max\{f,0\}
.

In particular, when
\psi(s)=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}
, the explicit entropy pair
is given by the mechanical energy and mechanical energy flux:

\displaystyle\eta_{E}(\rho,m)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+\rho e(\rho),\qquad q_{E}(\rho,m)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{3}}{\rho^{2}}+m\big(\rho e(\rho)\big)^{\prime},

(2.15)

where the internal energy
e(\rho)
is given by
relation
P(\rho)=\rho^{2}e^{\prime}(\rho)
with
e(0)=0
.
The relative mechanical energy with respect to the far-field state
(\rho_{\infty},0)
with
\rho_{\infty}>0

(
i.e.
,
(\rho_{0},m_{0})\to(\rho_{\infty},0)
as
|x|\to\infty
) is

\eta_{E}^{*}(\rho,m)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty}),

(2.16)

where the relative internal energy is defined by

e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty}):=\rho e(\rho)-\rho_{\infty}e(\rho_{\infty})-\big(\rho e(\rho)\big)^{\prime}|_{\rho=\rho_{\infty}}(\rho-\rho_{\infty}).

(2.17)

Notice that we have assumed that the far-field velocity
u_{\infty}=\frac{m_{\infty}}{\rho_{\infty}}=0

without loss of generality, due to the Galilean invariance.

There is also a high-order energy:

\eta_{\diamond}(\rho,m)=\frac{1}{12}\frac{m^{4}}{\rho^{3}}+\frac{e(\rho)}{\rho}m^{2}+g(\rho),

with
g(\rho)
determined by

g(0)=g^{\prime}(0)=0

and
g^{\prime\prime}(\rho)=\frac{2P^{\prime}(\rho)e(\rho)}{\rho}
,
for which the associated energy flux is denoted by
q_{\diamond}(\rho,m)
.
As above, the relative high-order energy is defined by

\eta_{\diamond}^{*}(\rho,m)=\frac{1}{12}\frac{m^{4}}{\rho^{3}}+\frac{e(\rho)}{\rho}m^{2}+e_{\diamond}^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty}),

where
e_{\diamond}^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})=g(\rho)-g(\rho_{\infty})-g^{\prime}(\rho_{\infty})(\rho-\rho_{\infty})
,
the associated entropy flux is denoted by
q_{\diamond}^{*}(\rho,m)
.

2.3
Properties of the General Pressure Function and
the Relative Internal Energy Function

We now present some properties of the pressure function
p=P(\rho)
determined by the general pressure law
(
2.3
)–(
2.7
) and the associated relative internal energy function.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
11
]
, Lemma 3.1)
.

For sufficiently small
\rho_{\star}
in (
2.4
) and sufficiently large
\rho^{\star}
in (
2.5
), the following properties hold
:

(i)

When
\rho\in\left(0,\rho_{\star}\right]
,

\displaystyle\underline{C}_{1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}}\leq P(\rho)\leq\overline{C}_{1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}},\quad\underline{C}_{1}\gamma_{1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}-1}\leq P^{\prime}(\rho)\leq\overline{C}_{1}\gamma_{1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}-1},

(2.18)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}-1}\leq e(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\gamma_{1}-1},\quad C^{-1}\rho^{\gamma_{1}-2}\leq e^{\prime}(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\gamma_{1}-2},

(2.19)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\rho^{\theta_{1}}\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\theta_{1}};

(2.20)

(ii)

When
\rho\in\left[\rho^{\star},\infty\right)
,

\displaystyle\underline{C}_{2}\rho^{\gamma_{2}}\leq P(\rho)\leq\overline{C}_{2}\rho^{\gamma_{2}},\quad\underline{C}_{2}\gamma_{2}\rho^{\gamma_{2}-1}\leq P^{\prime}(\rho)\leq\overline{C}_{2}\gamma_{2}\rho^{\gamma_{2}-1},

(2.21)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\rho^{\gamma_{2}-1}\leq e(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\gamma_{2}-1},\quad C^{-1}\rho^{\gamma_{2}-2}\leq e^{\prime}(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\gamma_{2}-2},

(2.22)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\rho^{\theta_{2}}\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)\leq C\rho^{\theta_{2}},

(2.23)

where, for
i=1,2
,
\,\,\theta_{i}=\frac{\gamma_{i}-1}{2}
,
the positive constants
\underline{C}_{i}
and
\overline{C}_{i}

depend only on
\gamma_{i}
respectively,
and
C=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star})>0
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The relative internal energy associated with the general pressure
law
(
2.3
)–(
2.7
)
satisfies the following properties
:

\displaystyle e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\geq C_{\gamma_{1}}\rho(\rho^{\theta_{1}}-\rho_{\infty}^{\theta_{1}})^{2}\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\text{ when $\rho\in\left(0,\rho_{\star}\right]$},

(2.24)

\displaystyle e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\geq C_{\gamma_{2}}\rho(\rho^{\theta_{2}}-\rho_{\infty}^{\theta_{2}})^{2}\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\text{ when $\rho\in\left[\rho^{\star},\infty\right)$},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma_{1}}\leq\bar{C}_{\gamma_{1}}\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{1}}\big)\qquad\qquad\text{when $\rho\in\left(0,\rho_{\star}\right]$},

(2.26)

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma_{2}}\leq\bar{C}_{\gamma_{2}}\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{2}}\big)\qquad\qquad\text{when $\rho\in\left[\rho^{\star},\infty\right)$},

(2.27)

\displaystyle\rho e(\rho)\leq\bar{C}\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}e(\rho_{\infty})\big)\quad\text{ when $\rho\in[\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star}]$},

(2.28)

where, for
i=1,2
,

\,\,\theta_{i}=\frac{\gamma_{i}-1}{2}
,

C_{\gamma_{i}}>0

are some constants,
\bar{C}_{\gamma_{i}}
are constants depending on
C_{\gamma_{i}}
,
and constant
\bar{C}=C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\gamma_{1},\kappa_{1})>0
.

These properties can be proved by direct calculation, so we omit the proof.

2.4
Stochastic Forcing

In this section, we present precise conditions on the stochastic forcing terms under consideration.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
be a stochastic basis
with complete and right-continuous filtrations.
Let
W(t)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\beta_{k}(t)e_{k}
, where
(\beta_{k}(t))_{k\geq 1}
are independent Brownian motions
relative to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(e_{k})_{k\geq 1}
denotes a complete orthonormal system
in a separable Hilbert space
\mathfrak{A}
. The noise coefficient
\Phi(\rho,m):\mathfrak{A}\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R})

satisfying
\Phi(0,m)=0
is defined by

\Phi(\rho,m)e_{k}=a_{k}\zeta_{k}(\cdot,\rho,m),

(2.29)

where
a_{k}
are constants such that
|a_{k}|
is monotone in
k
and
|a_{k}|\to 0
as
k\to\infty
,
and
\zeta_{k}
are continuous in
(\rho,m)
so that they satisfy the following condition uniformly
in
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{1}(x,\rho,m):=\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}|a_{k}\zeta_{k}(x,\rho,m)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}|\zeta_{k}(x,\rho,m)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq B_{0}\rho\Big(1+\big(\frac{m}{\rho}\big)^{2}+e(\rho)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\text{supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k})\subset\mathbb{K}\Subset\mathbb{R},

or

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{2}(x,\rho,m):=\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}|a_{k}\zeta_{k}(x,\rho,m)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}|\zeta_{k}(x,\rho,m)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\leq B_{0}\rho\Big(\big(\frac{m}{\rho}\big)^{2}+e(\rho)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\qquad\text{for any $x\in\mathbb{R}$},

where
C>0
and
B_{0}>0
are constants.

We define the auxiliary space
\mathfrak{A}\subset\mathfrak{A}_{0}
by

\mathfrak{A}_{0}=\big\{v=\sum_{k\geq 1}\alpha_{k}e_{k}\,:\,\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\alpha_{k}^{2}}{k^{2}}<\infty\big\},

with the norm:

\|v\|_{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}^{2}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\alpha_{k}^{2}}{k^{2}}\qquad\quad\mbox{for }\,v=\sum_{k\geq 1}\alpha_{k}e_{k}.

The embedding:
\mathfrak{A}\subset\mathfrak{A}_{0}
is Hilbert-Schmidt.
The trajectories of
W(t)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\beta_{k}(t)e_{k}

are
\mathbb{P}
-
a.s.
in
C([0,T];\mathfrak{A}_{0})
(see
[
36
]
).

Remark 2.1
.

A simple example of the noise coefficient
\Phi(\rho,m)
satisfying the conditions above
is given by

\Phi(\rho,m)e_{k}=\zeta_{k}(\cdot,\rho,m)=\begin{cases}a_{1}\alpha(x)\rho(x)\quad&\mbox{for $k=1$},\\
0,\quad&\mbox{for $k>1$},\end{cases}

where
\alpha(x)\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is a given function.

Remark 2.2
.

To understand the definition of the stochastic forcing terms,
we provide a simplified model of stochastic forcing terms here
:

Let the noise be a one-dimensional
(
1
-
D)
Wiener
process
(
Brownian motion
)
, i.e.,

W(t)=\beta_{1}(t)
.
In this case, the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{A}=\mathbb{R}
is a
1
-
D
space,
with the basis
e_{1}=1
. Then the noise coefficient is a function

\Phi(\rho,m,x):=a_{1}\alpha(x)\rho,

where
\alpha(x)\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is a given function.

2.5
Martingale Solutions and Main Theorems

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of martingale solutions with finite relative-energy
and the main theorems (Theorems 2.1–2.4) of this paper.

2.5.1
Martingale solutions with finite relative-energy

For the general pressure law
(
2.3
)–(
2.7
),
when the initial density has a positive far-field
\rho_{\infty}>0
,
we consider the following martingale
solutions.

Definition 2.1
.

We call

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t}),\mathbb{P},\rho,m,W)

a martingale
solution with finite relative-energy of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
if

(i)

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t}),\mathbb{P})
is a stochastic basis
with filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
satisfying the usual conditions
;

(ii)

W
is a
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-cylindrical Wiener process
;

(iii)

The density
\rho\geq 0
is predictable and

\rho\in C_{\rm w}([0,T],L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}))

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
;

(iv)

The momentum
m
is predictable and

m\in C_{\rm w}([0,T],L^{\frac{2\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{2}+1}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}))

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
;

(v)

The relative-energy is bounded:

\mathbb{E}\big[\big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\big\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\big]\leq C(T,E_{0,1}),

(2.32)

where
E_{0,1}
is the finite initial relative-energy
;

(vi)

For any
\varphi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R})
,
the mass and momentum equations hold almost surely
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho\partial_{t}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}m\partial_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=0,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}m\partial_{t}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\big)\partial_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi(U)\,\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(t)=0.

When the initial data satisfy

(\rho_{0},m_{0})\to(0,0)\ \text{ as $|x|\to\infty$},

we can formulate the definitions as above with apparent change.

Remark 2.3
.

In
Definition
2.1
,
the solutions are required to belong to the space
C_{\rm w}([0,T];X)
with
X
being a Banach space,
i.e., the solutions are continuous under the weak topology of
X
.
This ensures that the solutions are stochastic processes in the classical sense,
which is different from the deterministic case where we usually obtain
that the solutions of the Euler system belong to
L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
for some
p>1
.

Remark 2.4
.

Using the embedding
:

L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R})

for
\ell>\frac{1}{2}
,
we can check that, by conditions
(iii)
and
(v)
in

Definition
2.1
,
under assumption
(
2.30
)
or (
2.31
)
on the noise coefficient function
\Phi(\rho,m)\in L_{2}(\mathfrak{A},H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R}))
,
i.e., the Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
\mathfrak{A}
to
H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R})
,
the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\Phi(\rho,m)\,{\rm d}W

is a well-defined
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-martingale taking value in
H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R})
.
Indeed, take (
2.30
) for example,

\displaystyle\|\Phi(\rho,m)\|_{L_{2}(\mathfrak{A},H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\|a_{k}\zeta_{k}(\rho,m)\|_{H^{-\ell}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k}\|a_{k}\zeta_{k}(\rho,m)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq B_{0}\int_{\mathbb{K}}\rho\,{\rm d}x\int_{\mathbb{K}}\big(\rho+\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+\rho e(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}x.

For the general pressure law (
2.3
)–(
2.7
),
if
\rho\in L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})

and
\frac{m}{\sqrt{\rho}}\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
, then the claim follows
by using (
2.22
)
and the fact that
\rho\leq 1+\rho^{\gamma_{2}}
.

Remark 2.5
.

For the simplified model of stochastic forcing terms given
in
Remark
2.2
, we directly obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\Phi(\rho,m,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}^{2}\,{\rm d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\int_{\text{\rm supp}_{x}(\alpha)}|a_{1}\alpha|\rho\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{2}{\rm d}\tau\leq C(\alpha,t)\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\Big(\int_{\text{\rm supp}_{x}(\alpha)}\rho\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{2}.

Then, by similar argument to that in
Remark
2.4
,
we see that the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\Phi(\rho,m,x)\,{\rm d}W
is well-defined.

2.5.2
Main Theorems:
Theorem
2.1

and Theorem

2.2

For the general pressure law (
2.3
)–(
2.7
),
when the initial density has a positive far-field
\rho_{\infty}>0
,
we have the following main result:

Theorem 2.1
.

Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},m_{0})\in L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})

have the finite relative-energy
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0},m_{0}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{m_{0}^{2}}{\rho_{0}}+e^{*}(\rho_{0},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x<\infty.

Then there exists a martingale
solution with finite relative-energy to (
1.1
) in the sense of

Definition
2.1
.
Moreover, for any entropy pair
(\eta,q)
defined in (
2.13
)
with generating function
\psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that is convex and satisfies

\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\frac{\psi(s)}{|s|^{2-\delta}}=0,\quad\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\frac{\psi^{\prime}(s)}{|s|^{1-\delta}}=0\qquad\,\,\text{for $\,2>\delta>\frac{3}{2}(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}})$,}

(2.33)

the following entropy inequality is almost surely satisfied for any nonnegative

\varphi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\eta(U)\varphi_{t}+q(U)\varphi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{m}\eta(U)\Phi(U)\,\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(t)

(2.34)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{m}^{2}\eta(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k})^{2}(U)\,\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\geq 0.

Remark 2.6
.

For
Theorem
2.1
,

E_{0,1}
in (
2.32
) satisfies
E_{0,1}\equiv\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0},m_{0})
.

Remark 2.7
.

For the polytropic pressure case, because of (
7.18
),
we can obtain a better result than that in
(
2.34
)
;

see
Definition
7.1

for the details.

Furthermore, we have the following main theorem:

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},m_{0})\in L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})

satisfy
\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0},m_{0})<\infty
and the following additional
finite relative higher-order energy condition
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\diamond}(\rho_{0},m_{0}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{12}\frac{m_{0}^{4}}{\rho_{0}^{3}}+\frac{e(\rho_{0})}{\rho_{0}}m_{0}^{2}+e_{\diamond}^{*}(\rho_{0},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x<\infty.

Then

(i)

There exists a martingale
solution with finite relative-energy to (
1.1
) in the sense of

Definition
2.1
.
Additionally, the entropy inequality (
2.34
) is almost surely
satisfied with (
2.33
) replaced by

\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\frac{\psi(s)}{|s|^{3-\delta}}=0,\quad\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\frac{\psi^{\prime}(s)}{|s|^{2-\delta}}=0\,\,\,\qquad\mbox{for }\begin{cases}3>\delta>2\big(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\big)\quad&\text{for $\gamma_{2}\leq 2$},\\
3>\delta>1\quad&\text{for $\gamma_{2}>2$}.\end{cases}

(2.35)

(ii)

Let
U^{\delta}:=(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})
be a sequence of
martingale entropy solutions to (
1.1
) obtained in
(i)

corresponding to a sequence of initial data
(\rho_{0}^{\delta},m_{0}^{\delta})
satisfying

\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0}^{\delta},m_{0}^{\delta})+\mathcal{E}_{\diamond}(\rho_{0}^{\delta},m_{0}^{\delta})\leq C
,
where constant
C
is independent of
\delta
. Then

(a)

U^{\delta}
satisfies
that, for any
K\Subset\mathbb{R}
, there exists
C(K,p,T)>0

independent of
\delta
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K}\big(\rho^{\delta}P(\rho^{\delta})+\frac{|m^{\delta}|^{3}}{(\rho^{\delta})^{2}}+\eta_{\diamond}(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C(K,p,T)\qquad\mbox{for any $t\in[0,T]$},

so that
U^{\delta}:=(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})
are tight in

L^{\gamma_{2}+1}_{\rm w,loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\times L^{\frac{3(\gamma_{2}+1)}{\gamma_{2}+3}}_{\rm w,loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

and admit the Skorokhod representation
(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\tilde{m}^{\delta})
.

(b)

When
\gamma_{2}<2
, there exist random variables
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{m})
such that
almost surely
(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\,\tilde{m}^{\delta})\to(\tilde{\rho},\,\tilde{m})

almost everywhere and
(
up to a subsequence
)
almost surely
:

(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\,\tilde{m}^{\delta})\to(\tilde{\rho},\,\tilde{m})\qquad\text{in $L^{\bar{p}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\times L^{\bar{q}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})$}

for
\bar{p}\in[1,\gamma_{2}+1)
and
\bar{q}\in[1,\frac{3(\gamma_{2}+1)}{\gamma_{2}+3})
, and

(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{m})
is also a martingale entropy solution to (
1.1
),
i.e., almost surely satisfies the entropy inequality (
2.34
) with (
2.33
) replaced by (
2.35
).

Remark 2.8
.

When
\gamma_{2}<2
, we can choose
1>\delta>2(1-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}})
. In this case, we obtain
the entropy inequality for the mechanical energy, i.e., the physical entropy.

In fact, the two results above can be more general, extending to the case of random initial data.

Theorem 2.3
.

Assume that
\Im
is a Borel probability measure on
L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

\displaystyle{\rm supp}\,\Im=\big\{(\rho,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\,:\,\rho\geq 0\big\},

\displaystyle E_{p_{0}}(\Im):=\int_{L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})}\big|\mathcal{E}(\rho,m)\big|^{p_{0}}\,{\rm d}\Im(\rho,m)<\infty\qquad\mbox{for some $p_{0}\geq 6$}.

Then there exists a martingale
solution with finite relative-energy to (
1.1
) in the sense of
Definition
2.1

with initial law
\Im
, i.e., there exists a
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
(\rho_{0},m_{0})
such that
\Im=\mathbb{P}\circ(\rho_{0},m_{0})^{-1}
,
that is,
\Im(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}\{\omega\,:\,(\rho_{0},m_{0})(\omega)\in\cdot\}
,
for which we denote
E_{0,p_{0}}:=E_{p_{0}}(\Im)
.
Moreover, the same entropy inequality as (
2.34
) is almost surely satisfied.

Theorem 2.4
.

Assume that
\Im
is a Borel probability measure on
L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle supp}\,\Im=\big\{(\rho,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\,:\,\rho\geq 0\big\},

\displaystyle E_{2}(\Im)=\int_{L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})}\big|\mathcal{E}(\rho,m)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}\Im(\rho,m)<\infty,

\displaystyle\int_{L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\diamond}(\rho,m)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}\Im(\rho,m)<\infty.

Then

(i)

There exists a martingale
solution with finite relative-energy to (
1.1
) in the sense of
Definition
2.1
with initial law
\Im
.
Additionally, the entropy inequality (
2.34
) is almost surely satisfied with (
2.33
) replaced by (
2.35
)
;

(ii)

Let
U^{\delta}:=(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})
be a sequence of martingale entropy solution to
(
1.1
) obtained in
(i)
corresponding to a sequence of initial law

\Im^{\delta}
satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})}\big|\mathcal{E}(\rho,m)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}\Im^{\delta}(\rho,m)+\int_{L^{\gamma_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})}\big|\mathcal{E}_{\diamond}(\rho,m)\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}\Im^{\delta}(\rho,m)\leq C,

where constant
C>0
is independent of
\delta
.
Then
U^{\delta}
satisfies that, for any
K\Subset\mathbb{R}
,
there exists
C(K,p,T)>0
independent of
\delta
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K}\big(\rho^{\delta}P(\rho^{\delta})+\frac{|m^{\delta}|^{3}}{(\rho^{\delta})^{2}}+\eta_{\diamond}(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C(K,p,T)\qquad\mbox{ for any $t\in[0,T]$},

so that
U^{\delta}:=(\rho^{\delta},m^{\delta})
are tight in

L^{\gamma_{2}+1}_{\rm w,loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\times L^{\frac{3(\gamma_{2}+1)}{\gamma_{2}+3}}_{\rm w,loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

and admit the Skorokhod representation
(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\tilde{m}^{\delta})
. Moreover, when
\gamma_{2}<2
, there exist random variables
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{m})
such that
almost surely
(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\,\tilde{m}^{\delta})\to(\tilde{\rho},\,\tilde{m})

almost everywhere and
(
up to a subsequence
)
almost surely,

(\tilde{\rho}^{\delta},\,\tilde{m}^{\delta})\to(\tilde{\rho},\,\tilde{m})\qquad\,\,\text{in $L^{\bar{p}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\times L^{\bar{q}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})$}

for
\bar{p}\in[1,\gamma_{2}+1)
and
\bar{q}\in[1,\frac{3(\gamma_{2}+1)}{\gamma_{2}+3})
.
In addition,
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{m})
is also an entropy solution to (
1.1
),
i.e., almost surely satisfies the entropy inequality (
2.34
)
with (
2.33
) replaced by (
2.35
).

Remark 2.9
.

When
p_{0}=1
,
E_{1}(\Im):=E_{0,1}
has appeared in (
2.32
).

Remark 2.10
.

Since
Theorem
2.1

and
Theorem
2.2
are the direct corollaries of

Theorem
2.3

and
Theorem
2.4
, respectively,
it suffices
to prove

Theorem
2.3
and
Theorem
2.4
.

Remark 2.11
.

In
Theorem
2.3
, we require
p_{0}\geq 6
in order to pass to the limit in the entropy flux and derive the entropy inequality
;

see
Propositions
3.1
and

6.2
–
6.3
.

When the initial data satisfy

(\rho_{0},m_{0})\to(0,0)\ \text{ as $|x|\to\infty$}
,
we can also formulate the theorems as above with apparent changes.

In this paper, we require additional notation for subsequent development.

From now on,
we define
\theta(\rho)=\theta_{1}
when
\rho\leq\rho_{\star}
and
\theta(\rho)=\theta_{2}
when
\rho\geq\rho^{\star}
.
We use the notation
(\rho,m)\in H
for convenience instead of
(\rho,m)\in H\times H
,
where
H
is any function space.
We use
\lfloor a\rfloor
to denote the largest integer that no more than
a
.
The transpose of a vector
U
is denoted by
U^{\top}
.

\hat{K}\Subset\mathbb{R}
means that
\hat{K}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
Denote
\,\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\,
as the weak-* convergence and
\,\rightharpoonup\,
as the weak convergence.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
denotes the space of distributions. We use
B_{r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

to denote an open ball with radius
r
and centered at the origin.

\mathcal{L}(f)
denotes the law of
f
.
The vacuum region is denoted by

\mathcal{V}:=\{(\rho,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\,:\,\rho=0\}
,
and the non-vacuum set is denoted by

\mathfrak{T}=\{(\rho,m)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\,:\,\rho>0\}
.
The Sobolev space
W^{k,2}
is denoted by
H^{k}
.
We denote the dual of
W^{1,\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{O})
by
W^{-1,1}(\mathcal{O})
, where
\mathcal{O}
is an open bounded set.
We use
C_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})
to denote the space of functions that
are continuous on
\bar{\mathcal{O}}
and vanish on the boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}
. The norm of
C_{0}(\bar{\mathcal{O}})
is
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Denote
X^{*}
as the dual space of
X
, and

X_{\rm w}

as the topological space
X
equipped with the weak topology.

If
X
be a Banach space, then
C_{\rm w}([0,T],X)
denotes the set of functions
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T];X)

that are continuous in
t\in[0,T]
under the weak topology,

i.e.
, for any sequence
(t_{k})_{k}\subset[0,T]
satisfying
t_{k}\to t
,

\lim_{t_{k}\to t}\langle g,f(t_{k})\rangle=\langle g,f(t)\rangle\qquad\mbox{for any $g\in X^{*}\,$, {\it a.e.} $t\in[0,T]$}.

Also
f_{n}\to f
in
C_{\rm w}([0,T],X)
means that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\langle g,f_{n}-f\rangle|\to 0

for any
g\in X^{*}
(see also
[
6
, Definition 1.8.1]
). Denote
C^{\alpha}(0,T;X)
as
the set of functions
f:[0,T]\to X
that are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous under the norm topology.

For
0<s_{1}<1
and
1\leq r_{1}<\infty
, denote

W^{s_{1},r_{1}}(0,T;X):=\Big\{f\,:\,f\in L^{r_{1}}(0,T;X)\ \text{and}\ \int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\|f(t)-f(\tau)\|_{X}^{r_{1}}}{|t-\tau|^{r_{1}s_{1}+1}}\,{\rm d}t{\rm d}\tau<\infty\Big\},

where
X
is a Banach space. The norm of this space is defined by

\|f\|_{W^{s_{1},r_{1}}(0,T;X)}:=\big(\|f\|_{L^{r_{1}}}^{r_{1}}+\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s_{1},r_{1}}}^{r_{1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{r_{1}}},

with semi-norm

\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s_{1},r_{1}}}:=\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\|f(t)-f(\tau)\|_{X}^{r_{1}}}{|t-\tau|^{r_{1}s_{1}+1}}\,{\rm d}t{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{\frac{1}{r_{1}}}
.

3
Stochastic Parabolic Approximation

For any fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we consider the following approximation equations
to (
1.1
):

\begin{cases}{\rm d}U^{\varepsilon}+\partial_{x}F(U^{\varepsilon}){\rm d}t=\varepsilon\partial_{xx}U^{\varepsilon}{\rm d}t+\Psi^{\varepsilon}(U^{\varepsilon}){\rm d}W(t),\\
U^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=U^{\varepsilon}_{0},\end{cases}

(3.1)

with

\Psi^{\varepsilon}(U^{\varepsilon})=(0\,,\,\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U^{\varepsilon}))^{\top},

where
\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U^{\varepsilon}):\mathfrak{A}\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is defined as

\Phi^{\varepsilon}(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})e_{k}=a_{k}\zeta^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\,\cdot\,,\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})

with

\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}(\zeta_{k}\jmath^{\varepsilon})*\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}\qquad\quad&\text{if}\ k\leq\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor,\\
0\quad&\text{if}\ k>\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor,\end{cases}

such that

\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}
is the approximation to the identity and
\jmath^{\varepsilon}
is a truncation.
More precisely,

\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho,m)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}I_{1}(\frac{x}{\varepsilon})I_{2}(\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon})I_{3}(\frac{m}{\varepsilon}),

where
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
are the approximations to the identity on
\mathbb{R}
,
I_{2}
is the approximation
to the identity on
\mathbb{R}
with
\text{supp}\,I_{2}\subset[0,1]
.
Here we set
\zeta_{k}=0
for
\rho\leq 0
to define the convolution with respect to
\rho
, which is consistent with
assumption (
2.30
)
or (
2.31
):

•

when
\zeta_{k}
satisfies (
2.30
),

\jmath^{\varepsilon}:=\jmath_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m),

•

when
\zeta_{k}
satisfies (
2.31
),

\jmath^{\varepsilon}:=\jmath_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m)\jmath_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x),

where

\text{supp}(\jmath_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m))\subset\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}}:=\{(\rho,m)\,:\,-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}\leq w_{1}\leq w_{2}\leq\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}\}

for the Riemann invariants
(w_{1},w_{2})
as defined in
(
2.10
), and

\jmath_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\jmath(\varepsilon x)
with
\jmath(x)=1
if
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\jmath(x)=0
if
|x|>1
.
We set
\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}=c_{1}\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{1}}
, where
c_{1}>0
is a fixed constant, and
\alpha_{1}
satisfies (see Lemma
5.2
)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}<\theta_{2}\qquad\quad&\text{when $\gamma_{1}\leq 2$},\\
\alpha_{1}<\min\{\frac{1}{2},\theta_{2}\}\quad&\text{when $\gamma_{1}>2$}\end{cases}

for the general pressure law case.
Letting
\varepsilon
be sufficiently small, we can always choose

\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}\geq 1+(\rho_{\infty}+1)^{\theta_{2}}

(3.2)

such that the initial
data
U^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}}

(see Theorem
3.1

and Proposition
8.3
).
Additionally, we also choose
\varepsilon
sufficiently small such that (see Lemma
5.2
)

\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}\geq\rho_{\star}.

(3.3)

Denote
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}=\{x\,:\,|x|<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\}

and

\mathbb{K}^{\varepsilon}=\{y\,:\,|y-x|<\varepsilon\,\,\,\mbox{for any $x\in\mathbb{K}$}\}\subset\mathbb{K}^{1}
.
Then, defining
\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{2}^{\varepsilon}

as in
(
2.30
)–(
2.31
) with
\zeta_{k}
replaced by
\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\text{supp}\,\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{K}^{1}\times\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}},\qquad\text{supp}\,\mathfrak{G}_{2}^{\varepsilon}\subset\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\times\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho,m)\leq B_{0}\rho\Big(1+\varepsilon^{2}+\big(\frac{m}{\rho}\big)^{2}+e(\rho)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\,\,\mathfrak{G}_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho,m)\leq B_{0}\rho\Big(\varepsilon^{2}+\big(\frac{m}{\rho}\big)^{2}+e(\rho)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.5)

Additionally, we have the following Lipschitz condition:

\sum_{k}|\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}(U_{1})-\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}(U_{2})|^{2}\leq C(\varepsilon)|U_{1}-U_{2}|^{2}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for any $x\in\mathbb{R}$ and $U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}$}.

From now on, to be clearer, we use the following notation:

\displaystyle D_{x}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho,m):=\partial_{x}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{\rho,m}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}\cdot(\partial_{x}\rho,\partial_{x}m),\qquad D_{x}^{j}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho,m):=D_{x}^{j-1}(D_{x}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}).

3.1
Well-Posedness of the Stochastic Parabolic
Approximation

We first state our hypotheses for the far-field condition of the initial data
and the corresponding growth condition for the noise coefficient function.

Hypothesis 3.1
.

There are three cases for the initial data and the noise coefficient functions
of (
1.1
)
:

•

Case 1:

(\rho_{0},u_{0})\to(0,0)
as
|x|\to\infty
, and
\zeta_{k}
satisfy (
2.31
).

•

Case 2:

(\rho_{0},u_{0})\to(0,0)
as
|x|\to\infty
, and
\zeta_{k}
satisfy (
2.30
).

•

Case 3:

(\rho_{0},u_{0})\to(\rho_{\infty},0)
as
|x|\to\infty
with
\rho_{\infty}>0
,
and
\zeta_{k}
satisfy (
2.30
).

Correspondingly, we have the following hypotheses:

Hypothesis 3.2
.

We approximate the initial data of parabolic approximation (
3.1
)
as follows
:

•

\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}\to\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)
as
|x|\to\infty
for
Cases 1–2
,
so that
\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{\alpha_{0}}
for some
\alpha_{0}>0
satisfying

\alpha_{0}\gamma_{2}>1
, and

\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)<\rho_{\star},

(3.6)

by letting
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

•

\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}\to\rho_{\infty}
as
|x|\to\infty
for
Case 3
.

Then we have the following existence and uniqueness of solutions
to the parabolic approximation equations (
3.1
),
where
\rho_{\infty}
is used to denote constant
\rho_{\infty}
or
\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)

for simplicity.

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume that
(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}-\rho_{\infty},u^{\varepsilon}_{0})\in H^{5}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
,\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\omega)\geq c_{0}>0
a.e.
in
\mathbb{R}
for all
\omega\in\Omega
,
and
U^{\varepsilon}_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle U^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}}\ \mathbb{P}\text{-{\it a.s.}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(u^{\varepsilon}_{0})^{2}+e^{*}(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+e^{*}(\rho_{0},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{p}\big]\quad\mbox{for $\,1\leq p<\infty$}.

Then the parabolic approximation equations (
3.1
) admit a global unique
strong solution
U^{\varepsilon}
that is a predictable process and satisfies that, for any
T>0
,

(i)

(\rho^{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\infty},u^{\varepsilon})\in C([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{4}(\mathbb{R}))

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
;

(ii)

U^{\varepsilon}\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}}
and
\|\frac{1}{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})}\leq e^{C(T,c_{0},\varepsilon,\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon})}

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
;

(iii)

The parabolic approximation equations (
3.1
) hold in the classical strong sense
;

(iv)

The following uniform energy estimate holds for
1\leq p<\infty
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}(u^{\varepsilon})^{2}+e^{*}(\rho^{\varepsilon},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\big(\rho^{\varepsilon}e(\rho^{\varepsilon})\big)^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{x}\rho^{\varepsilon})^{2}+\rho^{\varepsilon}(\partial_{x}u^{\varepsilon})^{2}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\Big)^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(p,T,E_{0,p}),

(3.7)

where
E_{0,p}=\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+e^{*}(\rho_{0},\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\big)^{p}\big]
,
and
C(p,T,E_{0,p})
is independent of
\varepsilon
.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is postponed
to §
8
.
In this section below, we first derive some essential uniform estimates,
which will be used to establish the compensated compactness framework.

3.2
Uniform Estimates of the Stochastic Parabolic
Approximation

For simplicity, in this subsection, we omit superscript
\varepsilon
whenever no confusion arises.
First we rewrite (
3.1
) as

\begin{cases}{\rm d}\rho+\partial_{x}m\,{\rm d}t=\varepsilon\partial_{x}^{2}\rho\,{\rm d}t,\\[1.42262pt]
{\rm d}m+\partial_{x}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}t=\varepsilon\partial_{x}^{2}m\,{\rm d}t+\Phi^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m)\,{\rm d}W(t),\\[1.42262pt]
(\rho,m)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},m_{0}).\end{cases}

(3.8)

3.2.1
Higher integrability of the density

Proposition 3.1
.

The solution of the Cauchy problem (
3.1
) obtained
in
Theorem
3.1
satisfies that,
for any
K\Subset\mathbb{R}
,
there exists
C({\gamma_{2}},K,p,T,E_{0,3p})>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K}\rho P(\rho)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C(\gamma_{2},K,p,T,E_{0,3p})\qquad\mbox{for any $t\in[0,T]$}.

(3.9)

Proof.

Let
w(x)\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an arbitrary function
satisfying
0\leq w(x)\leq 1
and
w\equiv 1
on
K
. Theorem
3.1
(iii)
guarantees that, for any fixed
x
, the equations in (
3.1
) hold
as a finite-dimensional stochastic differential system.
This then enables us to calculate similarly to that in the deterministic case
[
15
, Lemma 3.3]
,
especially integrate the equation for
wm
on
(-\infty,x)
and finally obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w^{2}\rho P(\rho)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,{\rm d}\Big(w\rho\int_{-\infty}^{x}wm\,{\rm d}y\Big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w^{2}\rho^{2}\partial_{x}u\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau-\varepsilon\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}w\rho wm\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}wm\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{x}wm\,{\rm d}y\Big){\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{2}w\rho\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{x}wm\,{\rm d}y\Big){\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}\rho w\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau+\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\rho\int_{-\infty}^{x}w\Phi^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{7}J_{i},

where

f_{1}:=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\Big(\partial_{y}w\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)\big)-\varepsilon\partial_{y}w\partial_{y}m\Big)\,{\rm d}y.

For the stochastic integral
J_{7}
, by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\Big|\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\rho\int_{-\infty}^{x}\Phi^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m)w\,{\rm d}y\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(\tau)\Big|^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\rho\int_{-\infty}^{x}a_{k}\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\rho,m)w\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}x\Big)^{2}\,{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\rho\,\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{3}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(\gamma_{2},B_{0})\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\big(1+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{2}}+\rho u^{2}\big)\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{2p}\big]r^{\frac{p}{2}},

where, in the last inequality, we have
used (
2.22
),
(
2.27
),
(
3.5
),
and the fact that
\rho\leq 1+\rho^{\gamma_{2}}
.

All the other terms can be estimated similarly as
J_{7}
and that in the deterministic
case
[
15
, Lemma 3.3]
, so we omit the details.
Combining the above estimates and applying (
3.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w^{2}\rho P(\rho)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\frac{3\varepsilon}{8}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]+C({\gamma_{2}},w,p,T,E_{0,2p}).

(3.10)

Next, we deal with the first term on the right-hand side to close the estimate.
We make the following decomposition:

\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau=\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}I_{B(\tau,\omega)^{\rm c}}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}I_{B(\tau,\omega)}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau,

where
B(t,\omega):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\rho(t,x,\omega)>\rho^{\star}\}
.
Then, to deal with the second term,
we apply the iteration technique in
[
15
, Lemma 3.3]
.
Using (
2.27
) and
the Sobolev embedding:
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
(see
[
1
, Theorem 4.12]
), by (
2.5
), we calculate similarly
to that in
[
15
, Lemma 3.3]
to obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon C(t,w,\rho^{\star})+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B(\tau,\omega)}\big(1+\rho P(\rho)\big)w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle+C(T)\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{2}}\big)\,{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\rho e(\rho)\big)^{\prime\prime}(\rho_{x})^{2}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{3}.

Substituting this estimate for
\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\rho^{3}w^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau
in (
3.10
), we complete the proof.
∎

3.2.2
Higher integrability of the velocity

For the general pressure law case, there is no explicit formula for the entropy kernel,
which makes it difficult to obtain the higher integrability of the velocity.
We adopt the special entropy pairs constructed in
[
11
, §4]

to overcome this difficulty. The special entropy
\breve{\eta}
satisfies

\breve{\eta}(\rho,u)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\qquad\quad&\text{for $u\geq\mathcal{K}(\rho)$},\\[1.42262pt]
-\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}-\rho e(\rho)\quad&\text{for $u\leq-\mathcal{K}(\rho)$},\end{cases}

(3.11)

and
\breve{\eta}(\rho,u)
is the unique solution of the Goursat problem in the region

\{-\mathcal{K}(\rho)\leq u\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)\}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{\rho}^{2}\breve{\eta}-\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\rho)^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}\breve{\eta}=0,\\[1.42262pt]
\breve{\eta}(\rho,u)|_{u=\pm\mathcal{K}(\rho)}=\pm\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big).\end{cases}

(3.12)

We recall some useful properties for this special entropy pair
(\breve{\eta},\breve{q})
.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
11
]
, Lemma 4.1)
.

The special entropy pair
(\breve{\eta},\breve{q})
satisfies the following
:

(i)

When
|u|\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)
,

\displaystyle\breve{q}(\rho,u)\leq\begin{cases}C\rho^{\gamma(\rho)+\theta(\rho)}\qquad\quad&\text{for $\rho\leq\rho_{\star}$ or $\rho\geq\rho^{\star}$},\\
C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star})\quad&\text{for $\rho_{\star}\leq\rho\leq\rho^{\star}$},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\breve{\eta}(\rho,u)\leq\begin{cases}C\rho^{\theta(\rho)+1}\qquad\quad&\text{for $\rho\leq\rho_{\star}$ or $\rho\geq\rho^{\star}$},\\
C\rho^{1+M_{1}}\quad&\text{for $\rho_{\star}\leq\rho\leq\rho^{\star}$},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\breve{\eta}(\rho,u)\leq\begin{cases}C\rho^{\gamma(\rho)-1}\qquad\quad&\text{for $\rho\leq\rho_{\star}$ or $\rho\geq\rho^{\star}$},\\
C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star})\rho^{M_{1}}\quad&\text{for $\rho_{\star}\leq\rho\leq\rho^{\star}$},\end{cases}

where
\gamma(\rho)=\gamma_{1}
when
\rho\leq\rho_{\star}
and
\gamma(\rho)=\gamma_{2}
when
\rho\geq\rho^{\star}
,
and
\theta(\rho)=\frac{\gamma(\rho)-1}{2}
.

(ii)

When
|u|\geq\mathcal{K}(\rho)
,

\breve{q}(\rho,u)\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{3}.

(iii)

If
\breve{\eta}_{m}
is regarded as a function of
(\rho,m)
, then,
for
(\rho,m)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
,

|\partial_{m\rho}\breve{\eta}(\rho,m)|\leq C\rho^{\theta(\rho)-1},\ \qquad|\partial_{m}^{2}\breve{\eta}(\rho,m)|\leq C\rho^{-1}.

Note that, even though some properties in Lemma
3.1
are not covered by
[
11
, Lemma 4.1]
, they can be proved similarly
as that in
[
11
, Lemma 4.1]
.

We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
that may depend on
\rho_{\star}
and
\rho^{\star}
that

\big|\partial_{x}U\,\nabla^{2}\breve{\eta}\,(\partial_{x}U)^{\top}\big|\leq C\big((\rho e(\rho))^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}+\rho(\partial_{x}u)^{2}\big).

Proof.

The key of the proof is to estimate the second-order derivatives of
\breve{\eta}
, which are similar to that in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 4.1]
, so we omit it.
∎

The relative-entropy pair that we consider is as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}(\rho,m)=\breve{\eta}(\rho,m)-\breve{\eta}(\rho_{\infty},0)-\nabla_{(\rho,m)}\breve{\eta}(\rho_{\infty},0)\cdot(\rho-\rho_{\infty},\,m),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(\rho,m)=\breve{q}(\rho,m)-\nabla_{(\rho,m)}\breve{\eta}(\rho_{\infty},0)\cdot(m,\,\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+P(\rho)),

which is still an entropy pair of (
1.1
).

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}(\rho,m)+\rho|\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(\rho,m)|^{2}\leq C\eta^{*}_{E}(\rho,m).

(3.14)

When
|u|\geq\mathcal{K}(\rho)
,

\tilde{q}(\rho,m)\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{3}-C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\rho_{\infty})\big(1+\eta^{*}_{E}(\rho,m)\big).

(3.15)

The proof of (
3.14
) is similar to that in
[
17
, Proof of Proposition 4.1]
, using Lemma
3.1
(iii). (
3.15
) follows from direct calculation and Lemma
3.1
(i)-(ii). We omit the details.

Now we are ready to prove the higher integrability of the velocity:

Proposition 3.2
.

The solution of (
3.1
) obtained
in
Theorem
3.1
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{K}\rho|u|^{3}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C(K,p,T,\gamma_{2},E_{0,3p})\qquad\mbox{for any $t\in[0,T]$},

(3.16)

where
K\Subset\mathbb{R}
is any compact set, and
C(K,p,T,\gamma_{2},E_{0,3p})
is independent of
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1
(iii), we apply the Itô formula
to
\tilde{\eta}
to obtain

{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}(U)=-\partial_{x}\tilde{q}(U)\,{\rm d}t+\varepsilon\nabla\tilde{\eta}(U)\partial_{x}^{2}U\,{\rm d}t+\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}W(t)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}t.

Then integrating on
(0,t)\times(-\infty,x)
gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{q}(U)(x)\,{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{q}(U)(-\infty)\,{\rm d}\tau-\int_{-\infty}^{x}\big(\tilde{\eta}(U)(t)-\tilde{\eta}(U)(0)\big)\,{\rm d}y+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\nabla\tilde{\eta}(U)\partial_{y}^{2}U\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}W(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau.

We first estimate the first three terms on the right-hand side.
We use Lemma
3.1
(i) to estimate the first term,
use (
3.14
) for the second term,
use integration by parts and Lemma
3.2

for the third term, and then finally obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{q}(U)(x)\,{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\rho_{\infty})\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{2\rho}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)(\tau)\,{\rm d}y+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}\tilde{\eta}(U)(x)\,{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle+\varepsilon C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star})\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\Big(\big(\rho e(\rho)\big)^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{y}\rho)^{2}+\rho(\partial_{y}u)^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau+C(\rho_{\infty},\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star})t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}W(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau.

Now, let
w(x)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an arbitrary function satisfying
0\leq w(x)\leq 1
and
w\equiv 1
on
K
.
Then multiplying the above inequality by
w(x)
and integrating on
\mathbb{R}
give

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x)\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{q}(U)(x)\,{\rm d}\tau dx

(3.17)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{2\rho}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}\tilde{\eta}(U)\,{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}x

\displaystyle+\varepsilon C\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\Big((\rho e(\rho))^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{y}\rho)^{2}+\rho(\partial_{y}u)^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}x+Ct\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}W(\tau){\rm d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}x.

Applying integration by parts and (
3.14
), we have

\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{x}\tilde{\eta}(U)\,{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}x\leq C(w)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}\big(\frac{m^{2}}{2\rho}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau.

For the stochastic integral, it follows from the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(\tau)\Big|^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}|{\rm supp}(w)|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta})^{2}\,{\rm d}y\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(w)t^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta})^{2}\,{\rm d}y\Big)^{p}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y\Big)^{p}\big]\Big).

For the second term on the right-hand side of the above inequality, we only show the estimate for Case 3 (see Hypotheses
3.1
–
3.2
),
since the estimates for the other cases are similar and easier.
For Case 3, by assumption (
3.5
),
we obtain that, for
\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\sum_{k}\int_{{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\frac{1}{\rho}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]

(3.18)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(B_{0}^{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})|{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})|t+C\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})}B_{0}^{2}\big(\rho u^{2}+\rho^{\gamma_{2}}+e(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\big((1+\varepsilon^{2}+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{2}})|{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})|t\big)^{p}+C(p)\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\big(\rho u^{2}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\Big)^{p}\big],

where we have used
\rho\leq 1+\rho^{\gamma_{2}}
in the
first inequality and
used (
2.22
) and
(
2.27
)
in the last inequality.
For Case 3,

{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})\subset\mathbb{K}^{1}
, so that
the first term on the right-hand side of the above estimate is uniformly bounded
with respect to
\varepsilon
.
Case 2 can be treated similarly.
While, for Case 1,

{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})=\Lambda^{\varepsilon}
,
letting
i=1
when
\rho\leq\rho_{\star}
and
i=2
when
\rho\geq\rho^{\star}
,
then the first term on the right-hand side of the above
estimate (
3.18
) becomes

C(p)\big((\varepsilon^{2}+\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)^{\gamma_{i}})|{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})|t\big)^{p},

which is uniformly bounded with respect to
\varepsilon
, by choosing
\alpha_{0}
in
\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{\alpha_{0}}

such that
\alpha_{0}\gamma_{1}>1
and
\alpha_{0}\gamma_{2}>1
, respectively.
When
\rho\in[\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star}]
, we
use (
2.28
), recall that

\rho_{\infty}(\varepsilon)<\rho_{\star}
(see (
3.6
)), then utilize
(
2.19
) and
choose
\alpha_{0}
such that
\alpha_{0}\gamma_{1}>1
to obtain its uniform bound.
For the first term, we can use (
3.14
) to estimate it.
Thus, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}\tilde{\eta}(U)\Phi^{\varepsilon}(U)\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}x{\rm d}W(\tau)\Big|^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C(w,p)t^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(1+\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\big(\rho u^{2}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\Big)^{p}\big]\Big).

Noticing that
|\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)|\leq\frac{1}{\rho}
. Therefore, similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\partial_{m}^{2}\tilde{\eta}(U)\sum_{k}a_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}\,{\rm d}y{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}x\leq C(w)t\Big(1+\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\int_{{\rm supp}_{x}(\zeta_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\big(\rho u^{2}+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})\big)\,{\rm d}x\Big).

Substituting the above estimates in (
3.17
),
taking the
p
-th power and the expectation, and applying
the energy estimate (
3.7
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{q}(U)\,{\rm d}\tau dx\Big)^{p}\big]\leq C(w,p,T,E_{0,p}).

(3.19)

By (
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|u|\geq\mathcal{K}(\rho)}\rho|u|^{3}w\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{q}(\rho,m)w\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau+2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}(w)}C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\rho_{\infty})\big(1+\eta^{*}_{E}(\rho,m)\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau.

When
|u|\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)
, for
\rho\leq\rho_{\star}
,
it follows from (
2.20
)
and (
2.26
)
that

\rho|u|^{3}\leq C\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}^{\gamma_{1}}\big).

For
\rho\geq\rho_{\star}
, it follows from
(
2.21
),
(
2.23
), and
\gamma_{2}<3
, that

\rho|u|^{3}\leq C\rho^{\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\big(1+\rho P(\rho)\big).

For
\rho_{\star}\leq\rho\leq\rho^{\star}
, it follows from
(
2.28
) that

\rho|u|^{3}\leq C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\rho_{\infty})\rho e(\rho)\leq C(\rho_{\star},\rho^{\star},\rho_{\infty})\big(e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho_{\infty}e(\rho_{\infty})\big).

Therefore, we obtain

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|u|\leq\mathcal{K}(\rho)}\rho|u|^{3}w\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\rm supp}w}\big(1+e^{*}(\rho,\rho_{\infty})+\rho P(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau.

Thus, combining the above estimates and using Proposition
3.1
, we conclude (
3.16
).
∎

4
Tightness of the Parabolic Approximation

In this section, we prove the tightness of
the parabolic approximation.
We first follow the notions introduced in Ball
[
2
]
. Denote
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
denote the space of bounded Radon measures
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
,

\mathcal{M}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
denote the space of nonnegative bounded Radon measures
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
,
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
denote the Banach space of continuous real functions
f

satisfying
\lim_{|x|\to\infty}f(x)=0
, and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

denote the set of all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
is identified with the dual of
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
by the duality pairing:

\langle\mu,f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}f\,{\rm d}\mu\qquad\mbox{for $\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})$ and $f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})$}.

(4.1)

In this paper,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
is equipped with the weak-* topology.
The norm on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
is given by
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{M}}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\,{\rm d}|\mu|
.
The space
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
is a Banach space of equivalence
classes of weak-* measurable mapping
\mu:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

that are essentially bounded, endowed with the norm

\|\mu\|_{\infty,\mathcal{M}}:={{\rm ess}\sup}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|\mu_{\mathbf{x}}\|_{\mathcal{M}}.

Here we call
\mu
is weak-* measurable if
\langle\mu_{\mathbf{x}},f\rangle
is measurable
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
for every

f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))

is a space of Young measures equipped with the same topology
as
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
.
The Young measure associated to a measurable map
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is the Dirac mass
given by
\delta_{h(\mathbf{x})}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
.
The space
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
is isometric isomorphic to the dual of

L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
via the duality pairing:

\langle\mu,g\rangle_{L^{\infty},L^{1}}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\langle\mu_{\mathbf{x}},g\rangle\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\qquad\mbox{for $\mu\in L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))$
and $g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))$}.

For
\mu\in L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
, we also use the notation
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{f}):=\mu(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{f})
, where
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}

such that
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.

From now on,
the space
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
and hence
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
are equipped with the weak-* topology.
Since
\|\nu\|_{\infty,\mathcal{M}}=1
for any Young measure
\nu\in L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
,
by Banach-Alaoglu’s theorem,
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))

is relatively compact in
L^{\infty}_{\rm w}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}))
.
However, we should note that the limit of the Young measures is not necessarily a Young measure.
We now provide a compactness criterion for the Young measures.
There are various results of this form such as
[
3
, Proposition 4.1]
and
[
21
, Theorem 5]
,
among others. Here our compactness criterion is similar to the one in
[
3
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\varsigma\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2};\mathbb{R}_{+})
satisfy the growth condition

\lim_{\mathbf{f}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},|\mathbf{f}|\to\infty}\varsigma(\mathbf{f})=\infty.
